```
scripts/checks/durations.ts          # run by the same script: findDurations, formatDuration, formatClock
scripts/checks/pinchOrganize.ts      # run by the same script: collection labels and keys, the filter's options and counts, the drawer's chip window, the suggestions at their edges, the form's choice, the delete's chunks and re-read, favorites order and the tap guard, the search by collection name, and the rules' caps
```

No `functions/src/apps/pinch/`: the only function Pinch calls is the shared `fetchPageContent`.

**Wiring checklist** (everything a new mini-app has to touch; each item is in the roadmap):

- `src/lib/types/appCatalog.ts`: `'pinch'` in `AppId`. `src/lib/app/app.registry.ts`: a registry entry (`path: '/pinch'`).
- `src/routes/AppRoutes.tsx`: lazy route to `@apps/pinch/Pinch`.
- A logo and a banner under `public/logos` and `public/banners/by-app`, the entry in `cloudflare-worker.js`, and the root `README.md` "Current apps" list (the registry's own comment lists exactly these).
- `src/store/index.ts`: `pinch` reducer and `RootState`.
- `firestore.rules` and `storage.rules`: the blocks above in alphabetical position, with emulator verification noted in the PR; `firestore.indexes.json` unchanged unless a query needs a composite index (prepared containers' Finished view adds one, decision 23), and the PR says which.
- `.github/instructions/pinch.instructions.md` and the identical `.claude/rules/pinch.md`.
- `scripts/seeds/pinch.ts` and `pinchScale.ts`, the `'pinch'` value in `SeedScope`, an `npm run seed:pinch` script, and the app's `firestoreDocuments` count.
- `SITE_VERSION` bumped (minor) in `src/lib/app/app.constants.ts` in each PR; `README.md`, `UX.md`, `TECHNICAL.md` current.

---

## Open questions for the roadmap

Each has a default the design already assumes; the owner can change any of them cheaply now and expensively later.

1. **Store logo source: Logo.dev is wired, its terms are still to be confirmed.** Logos load by domain in the person's browser behind `storeLogoUrl` (enabled only when `VITE_STORE_LOGO_TOKEN` is set, with a letter-tile fallback and `no-referrer`). Logo.dev's Community plan needs attribution for commercial projects and says logos remain their owners' trademarks; the plan's terms and the attribution wording were not verified from the sandbox. Gate before launch: read them, then keep or reword the attribution line already under "About the numbers" in View options, or leave the token unset. Fallback if it doesn't fit: letter tiles plus person-uploaded images only.
2. **USDA FoodData Central licence** is to be confirmed before the catalog ships (the page could not be read while planning). Defaults to FDC's public-domain/CC0 position; the generated catalog keeps a `source` note per row.
3. **Ingredient icons: removed, approach to be decided.** The vendored Twemoji set, `IngredientIcon` and the icon picker are gone, and `IngredientRecord.iconKey` stays in the type as `@deprecated` (existing documents may carry one; new writes null). Whatever returns (a licensed set, emoji per family, or nothing) will need its own notice if vendored.
4. **The calendar grid's first weekday: decided.** Dreamer UI's `Calendar` has no week-start option (its weekday header and `daysInView` are Sunday-first), so the month grid starts on Sunday, and the wide week's columns and `getWeekStartKey` start on Sunday to match it; below `lg` the week starts today instead, with this week's passed days folded above it. Building a Monday-first grid by hand would give up the shared `Calendar`; it is cheap to revisit if the owner wants it.
5. **Freezing from first-load data.** A past meal is frozen from the data at the first load after its day, with a freeze-before-edit step. Prices changed while the app wasn't opened for a month or more can leak into meals that were never frozen. Alternative: a daily scheduled Function that freezes by UTC day (adds a Function, a secret-free deploy, and a time-zone approximation).
6. **"Average per fully priced meal" divides only meals with nothing left out**, from three of them, and "price per gram of protein" uses only those meals where protein is known. An average over meals with any price was an average of floors (a week where 2 of 20 meals were fully priced read "$0.30 a meal"), so the tile names its base instead. Both are my choices.
7. **Pinch uses `kitchens/{uid}` private data only.** If "cook with someone" means a live shared kitchen (not a one-time copy of a plan), the shared data needs its own tree; the `kitchens` root makes that a read grant on this document for the first version but not a co-edited kitchen.
8. **`refIds` is a client-written index** the rules don't verify. A wrong value can only make a delete hard-delete something still in use; the single writer is the owner. The defence is the client derive-on-write and the emulator checks.
9. **No test runner exists in the repo.** The quantity bridge, cost and stats math are pure and the most valuable thing to test. Default: `npm run check:pinch-math` (`scripts/checks/pinchMath.ts`, plain `node:assert`) runs the cost worked examples above and the nutrition-basis conversions (per 28 g, per ounce, per serving, an unweighable serving), plus the timezone-pinned browser pass the repo already requires. CI runs it, and `npm run check:pinch-ingest` (the recipe-reading normalizer and ingredient matching), on every pull request that touches `src`, `scripts/checks` or `scripts/seeds`: the `check_pinch` job in `.github/workflows/check-firestore-indexes.yml`, on Node 20, with the math checks under UTC, America/Los_Angeles and Pacific/Auckland. It sits beside `check_indexes` so that job's name is unchanged.
10. **Unit conversions beyond the catalog are Next Steps.** A custom ingredient has no density or per-piece weight in the MVP, so a recipe line in cups of a custom ingredient priced by the pound is left out and named, and its nutrition can be typed per gram or ounce but not per a serving that can't be weighed (a cup, "each"). The `gramsPerMl` and `gramsEach` fields are in the schema already so adding the UI later needs no migration.
11. **Ingredient serving sizes.** An ingredient item in a meal ("1 serving of Doritos") needs a serving size; the catalog supplies one and a custom ingredient defaults to 100 g until the person sets one. Doritos-style items need that field in the ingredient form's extras, where it also lets the label's nutrition be typed per serving.
12. **The AI text cap** (20,000 characters) and the 10 MB upload cap are reasonable limits set here, not provider limits.
13. **Reading a recipe from a link is unverified from the sandbox.** A real page fetch through `fetchPageContent` and the AI step over a page's recipe data had no outside network to run against; the contract (schema.org nodes in `structuredData`, capped `text`, the page's `meta`) is from the code and docs, so check a few real recipe sites before relying on it, including how well the model reads a page that publishes no Recipe data and only has text. The page reader itself was exercised on sample and hostile HTML, and the callable's validation and private-address refusals against the emulator.
14. **Recipe sources: a few things are unverified or accepted.** (a) A recipe read from a link starting on its site's source was checked in the browser with an injected reading and against the host-matching helper, but a real link reading (the page fetch and the AI step) had no outside network to run against. (b) A source's label is unique only as far as the loaded list: two devices adding the same label at once can leave two, which the person can rename or delete; making it strict would need the normalized label as the document id. (c) Deleting a source with more than 400 recipes commits several batches that are not atomic with one another; the source goes with the first, and any recipe left pointing at it reads as having none. (d) The rules and the client ship together: a client that writes `sourceId` against rules that haven't deployed is refused (`hasOnly`), so the rules go out first, as for every field added to a recipe.
15. **The shopping list: a few things are unverified or accepted.** (a) The cost line prices the amount on the list from each ingredient's default product, not whole packages, so it is lower than a till total; the line says what it covers ("About $3.16 for 3 of 4 things") and shows only when it has a figure (nothing is drawn below half priced or with nothing priced), its help icon says that stores sell whole packages and the till total is usually more, and what it can't price is named with where to fix it. (b) 200 lines per add and 300 items on the list are limits of the action and the screens, not of the rules (§5b). (c) A change of unit is one transaction, but a unit that the same ingredient already has adds to it when it is open and restarts it when it is ticked, and the sheet says which before saving. (d) The rules and the client ship together: every add writes `sources`, and a name-only line is a new shape, so against rules that haven't deployed every shopping add and Undo is denied (and the list may say it couldn't load); the preview build, or a stale tab running the new client, talks to the production backend, so it can't exercise the list until the rules are live (CI deploys them on merge). (e) Adding needs a connection because a transaction does not queue offline; this was reasoned from how Firestore transactions work and not tried on a device with the network off. (f) A line's sources say what put it there since it was last cleared, not which of them its open amount is for: a ticked line that an add starts again keeps its old sources, so the "for …" line can name a meal whose share was already bought, and removing that meal takes its share off the restarted amount (clamped at zero). A source whose planned meal is outside the loaded months reads like a deleted one (dropped from the "for …" line and from Meals on your list) until the calendar loads it. (g) Removing a meal from the list works from the meal as it is now and needs a connection (§5b "What the list is for, and stopping one meal"); it was checked in the browser and against the emulator rules, and its offline line reads `navigator.onLine`, which a captive portal can fool, in which case the transaction fails after its retries with "Check your connection and try again."
16. **Recipe ideas: a few things are unverified or accepted.** (a) A real page lookup had no outside network to run against. The success path was driven in the browser with the callable's answer stubbed (including a picture on an http address, 300-character titles and a response slower than the timeout), the failure path against the Functions emulator (which cannot reach a site), and the contract (`title` from the page's `<title>`, `meta.siteName` from `og:site_name`, `meta.image` from `og:image` or `twitter:image`) from reading `fetchPageContent`; check a few real recipe sites. A page's `<title>` often ends in the site's name ("Lemon bars | Serious Eats"); it is kept as it is and the person can change it. (b) A picture that only has an http address is not kept (the rules and the client want https). (c) Offline, a lookup fails and the idea saves with the host as its title, and the final `setDoc` queues. A Storage upload doesn't queue: it retries up to the SDK's limit and then fails with the "timed out, check your connection" sentence, and any other Storage error (permission, quota, an unknown code) gets its own plain sentence from `getStorageErrorMessage` and never says to check the connection. How long the upload retries offline is reasoned from the SDK, not verified. (d) The 200-idea cap is the action's and the screens', not the rules'. (e) A photo idea's picture is not copied to a recipe made from it, so that recipe has no cover; the idea keeps its photo. (f) Changing an idea's link doesn't look the new page up: the old picture is dropped and the site becomes the host. (g) The rules and the client ship together: a client that writes an idea against rules that haven't deployed is refused and the Ideas list says it couldn't load them; the preview build talks to the production backend, so it can't exercise ideas until the rules are deployed (CI deploys them on merge, and `storage.rules` carries the photo path). (h) The idea's link to its recipe is its own write after the recipe commits, so an idea deleted on another device a moment earlier costs only the link: the recipe saves, the toast is the usual "Added X to your recipes" with no mention of the idea, and the failed write is logged (§5c). The batch queued offline therefore never holds an idea document. If the idea was already gone from the loaded store the link is not even tried. (i) Photo objects can be left behind, and are accepted: when an idea is deleted offline its Storage delete is best effort and may not run, and when replacing a photo the object is overwritten before the document write, so a failed write leaves the new picture under the old document. Both are private to the person, at most 5 MB, one object per idea path, and nothing points at a stray one. (j) `toWebLink` picks the one link out of pasted text that has words around it, but a link with no scheme and spaces among other words ("Lemon bars example.com/x") is joined into one host, because a link wrapped over lines has the same shape; a link that starts with a scheme and wrapped over lines is cut at the first break. (k) One Escape closes a destructive confirm and the edit sheet under it together, as it does for every existing form sheet; the back gesture closes the open drawer or sheet first (Component Encapsulation, "The back gesture, one layer at a time").
17. **Cooking mode: a few things are unverified or accepted.** (a) Wake Lock was checked against a stub that mimics a browser releasing the lock when the page is hidden; headless Chromium refuses the real request, and iOS Safari's support (and its behaviour in an installed web app) was not tried. (b) Vibration, the beep and the toast were checked by counting calls with stubs and with Playwright's clock; no real device buzzed or beeped, and iOS plays sound from a page only after a tap, which the first timer's start provides. (c) Timers were fast-forwarded with a fake clock and a hidden-then-visible tab; a real phone put to sleep or a real backgrounded tab was not tried, and a locked phone will not ring. (d) The time reader is English only and reads a range as its longer end, an unusual phrasing ("a couple of minutes", "overnight", "until golden") gets no chip, and a person can still use their own timer. (e) The ingredients view shows the recipe's lines as written, so two lines for the same ingredient and unit stay two (the shopping list merges them). (f) Amounts inside a step's text are not scaled. (g) Announcements and focus were checked by reading the live region's text and `document.activeElement` after each action; no screen reader (VoiceOver, TalkBack, NVDA) was used. (h) A backwards or forwards clock step was simulated with Playwright's `page.clock.setSystemTime`, which keeps timers on their own clock like a browser does; a real device clock change was not tried. (i) Pinch and zoom were checked through CDP touch events and `Emulation.setPageScaleFactor` (the computed `touch-action` is `pan-y pinch-zoom` and a swipe is ignored while zoomed), not with a real two-finger pinch on a phone. (j) The timer view's digit sizes were measured in headless Chromium, whose fallback sans is wider than a phone's system font, so a phone has more room than measured; at the four-timer cap on a 568px phone the view scrolls (its footer stays pinned and every row's own control was reached), and a finish that lands while a note is being typed waits, lit in the pill row, until the note is saved or cancelled. (k) The repeating alert, the age line and the tap guard were checked with Playwright's clock and a stubbed `navigator.vibrate` (one buzz at the finish, one more a minute later, none after the fifth repeat, none after Dismiss); no real device buzzed or beeped again, and a hidden tab's throttled timeout fires once on return rather than once per missed minute. (l) With a Time's up undismissed the timer view's age line moves on by a timeout aimed at the next minute, so a tab returned to after a long hide shows the old age until that timeout fires.
18. **Cooking history: a few things are unverified or accepted.** (a) The 500-cook cap is the app's, not the rules' (§5d). (b) A transaction doesn't queue offline, so marking from a planned meal needs a connection; marking from the recipe is an ordinary write and queues. Both were reasoned from how the SDK behaves, not tried with the network off. (c) Deleting a recipe removes its cooks (read from Firestore, not the store) in batches, as best effort; a failed or partial cascade leaves unreachable documents, which readers ignore and nothing cleans up later. (d) `cookedAt` is checked against the server's clock plus a day in the rules and against `useNow()` (a 15-second clock) in the form, so a time within the same quarter-minute can be refused for a moment. (e) A planned day marked cooked on another device keeps its cook if the planned meal is removed; nothing links them after that. (f) The rules and the client ship together: a client that writes a cook against rules that haven't deployed is refused, and the drawer then offers no "Mark as cooked"; the preview build talks to the production backend, so it can't exercise cooks until the rules are deployed (CI deploys them on merge). (g) One Escape closes a destructive confirm and the history sheet under it together, as it does for every existing form sheet; the back gesture closes the open drawer or sheet first (Component Encapsulation, "The back gesture, one layer at a time"). (h) A reminder toast's "View" only dismisses while a dialog is open (`hasDialogOnTop()` in `useReminderToasts`), so tapping it can't navigate away from a form modal holding typed text; with no dialog it navigates as before. The toast stack's `z-index` is a central rule that reaches every mini-app: toasts now sit above an open drawer or modal instead of behind it, readable and tappable (checked with Waypoint's reminder toasts over its New trip modal: behind the overlay and untappable before, on top and tappable after). The cost is that on a phone a stack of toasts can cover the top of a modal, its close button included, until they time out or are swiped away.
19. **Recipe notes: a few things are unverified or accepted.** (a) There is no way to save an edit without its tweak: fixing a typo in a cooked recipe still adds a note ("Description changed"). An opt-out on the form's "What changed and why?" line would cost one more control and a way to say it; the default keeps the history complete. (b) A tweak's lines are compared with the recipe as this device last had it, because the save is a batch (as specified), not a transaction that re-reads the recipe; two devices editing the same recipe at once can each record lines that don't match the other's. (c) The 300-note cap is the app's, not the rules'; at 300 an edit saves without a tweak and the form says so. (d) A recipe with no cooks records nothing, even when the form was opened from a note; the quote shows, the "What changed and why?" line doesn't, and the note's words are not carried over. (e) One Escape over the delete confirm closes the confirm and the sheet beneath it, as it does over the cooking history's delete (the drawer and the confirm both listen for the key), and the back gesture closes the sheet first (Component Encapsulation, "The back gesture, one layer at a time"); the double close on Escape is not new, and was checked, not fixed. (f) The tweak lines are English only and name ingredients as they are called when the recipe is saved, so renaming an ingredient later does not rewrite an old tweak. (g) A device with a far-future clock writes a tweak whose `createdAt` is in the future, and later notes then read "Written before your <that day> tweak". The rules do not bound `createdAt` or `updatedAt`, because bounding the tweak would fail the whole recipe save on a skewed device (cooks do bound `cookedAt`).

20. **Recipe care: a few things are unverified or accepted.** (a) The three readers were exercised with the model's answer stubbed (the request's schema and prompt, a reading with storage lines, one without, one with out-of-range and wrong-typed values, 84 days as "12 weeks", and all three ways); how well the real model reads a storage line, and converts weeks and months, on a real page, photo or PDF had no outside network to run against, so check a few real recipes, including a page that gives a range ("3 to 4 days", read as 3) or only a freezer time. (b) Care is days only, 1 to 365: a freezer measured in years is not representable, and a month is fixed at 30 days (so "3 months" is 90, not 91 or 92). (c) A range reads as its shorter end; this is a product choice for a food-safety figure and the opposite of cooking mode's timers, which take the longer end of a cooking range. (d) Care edits never record a tweak, even on a cooked recipe: storage advice isn't a change to what is cooked or how, and a tweak moves the drawer's "changed" date to today and makes every older note read "Written before your <today> tweak" although the dish didn't change. The cost is that a care edit leaves no dated trace. The alternative is to compare care in `getRecipeChanges` (lines such as "Fridge storage 3 days → 5 days") so it is recorded like any other edit; it is a product decision and reversing it means adding that comparison back, with its lines in form order after Steps. (e) Cooking mode's line speaks only of days; reheating and a tip show on the recipe's drawer, not on the last screen. (f) There is no copy-out of recipes in Pinch to carry care, so the "copy" part of the idea has nothing to attach to until one returns. (g) Rules and client ship together, as for every field added to a recipe: the recipe's save writes `care` on every save, so a new client against rules that haven't deployed is refused for every recipe save, not only for ones that use care; the preview build talks to the production backend, so it can't save a recipe until the rules are deployed (CI deploys them on merge), and the rules should go out first. (h) The editor opens as a popover from `sm` and is about 370px tall, taller than the other row editors: near the bottom of a short window the popover runs past the screen edge and the form has to be scrolled to reach Done, as Description's would in the same place, and it stays reachable because the popover scrolls with the form. (i) The reader is told not to estimate, but nothing in the app can tell a stated time from a guessed one; the person reviews every filled value before saving.

21. **Pricing a past day again: a few things are unverified or accepted.** (a) The owner chose a one-tap re-price of a whole day from today's prices over per-product price history; the day's nutrition is read again with its prices (it is one `buildSnapshot`), so a recipe edited since the day passed can move a day's calories along with its cost, and the row can appear on a day that used such a recipe with no price changed. The screen says so: the row's subtitle ("Redoes this day's cost and nutrition from today") and the confirm, which previews the total before and after and says it redoes the day's cost and nutrition from today's prices and recipes, both hold for a day that had no prices too. (b) A day is re-priced as a whole: a meal whose own numbers wouldn't change is still given a fresh snapshot, so a day never reads as part re-priced; a day with more than 400 meals refuses with a sentence, and the row isn't offered for one. A re-price that finds nothing to change (another device did it a moment earlier) writes nothing and toasts "already uses today's prices". (c) A priced line can become unpriced if what it named has been removed since (ingredients and recipes in use are archived rather than removed, so this needs data that was deleted from outside the app), and the confirm already says the old prices are replaced. (d) The prices and recipes come from the loaded store, the meals from the transaction's own reads, so a price changed on another device a moment earlier and not yet synced isn't in the numbers. (e) A transaction needs a connection, so re-pricing offline fails with a sentence and writes nothing; this was reasoned from how transactions work and not tried with the network off. (f) The rules and the client ship together: the client writes `repricedAt: null` in every snapshot it freezes or plans onto a past day, which a deployed backend that predates the key refuses (`hasOnly`), and the freeze then fails without showing anything (`freezeBeforeEdit` swallows it), so a price or recipe edit made in that window leaves past days unfixed and reading live prices until a freeze succeeds after the rules deploy; the preview build talks to the production backend, so it can't freeze or re-price until the rules are deployed (CI deploys them on merge), and the rules go out first.

22. **The fridge and freezer (containers in the code): a few things are decided here, and a few are unverified or accepted.** (a) The history holds ten events, not more, because the rules evaluator allows 1,000 expressions per request and a list can only be walked by index (criterion 13); past ten the oldest eats go first, then the oldest eat-by edits, then the oldest move after the first place event that is not a freeze, so a container eaten a portion at a time over more than ten sittings loses its earliest EATs while its place events stay (and the count of portions is the document's own `servings`); only a long run of moves (frozen and thawed five times over) can drop the oldest thaws, never the first place event or a freeze. Merging eats of the same day into one event would stretch this and would need the undo to subtract a count; not done. (b) What a bulk meal is (five or more servings, a recipe that says it freezes, or one already in stock) is a rule of mine, stated in one place (`isBulkMeal`); the owner may want a different threshold. (c) The default eat-by days (4 for the fridge, 90 for the freezer, 2 once thawed) are named starting points, not advice, and the copy says "Your call". (d) The Finished view's composite index is declared but the emulator never enforces indexes, so it is unverified until the index is built after deploy; until then the Finished view shows one line saying it couldn't load. (e) OWNER DECISION, thawing portions: real meal prep puts portions in separate boxes and thaws one the night before, but a put-away used to be one container of N servings, so "Thaw in the fridge" moved all N. It is solved by containers × servings each: "How many boxes?" (1 to 20) splits the servings evenly (the extra ones go one each to the first) into that many containers in one batch, each its own history, with the same cook and eat-by, so one box thaws alone. A container already put away whole is not split after the fact; whether the owner wants "split this container" is open. (f) OWNER DECISION, planning from stock: food eaten from a container still counts its cost in the plan's tiles, the shopping sheet still pre-ticks a recipe's ingredients (it only says you already have servings), a plan can't be built from stock ("use the lentil soup on Tuesday"), and there is no reminder and no Calendar surface of its own for what to eat soon (a planned meal with a matching box due today or tomorrow offers "Eat from fridge" as its primary, `plannedMealOffer`). These are follow-ups for the owner to decide, not part of this change. (g) The rules were proven on the emulator against the previous rules: the 37 new-behaviour rows are owner-only and every other row, for every existing collection, is identical (432 cases a file); the evaluator's expression limit in production is assumed to be the emulator's, since it is the same engine. (h) Deploy order: the client writes only the new collection, so against rules that predate this PR the listener is denied and the container UI says so in one line while nothing else changes; the preview channel builds against the production rules and CI deploys rules only on merge, so it can't exercise containers until they are deployed. (i) No push or reminder for an approaching eat-by date, no automatic eating and no subtracting from the shopping list: follow-ups. (j) A saved meal's container counts plates and is eaten one at a time from a plan; a recipe's counts its own servings. (k) A typo in a typed container's name can only be fixed by deleting it; `title` is editable in the rules but no screen edits it yet.

23. **Ingredient groups: a few things are decided here, and a few are unverified or accepted.** (a) The rules can't check a line's group within the expression budget (Security Rules, criterion 14), so its 40-character cap and trimming are the client's. (b) The readers were exercised through `normalizeExtractedRecipe` and `getDataFromExtracted` with the model's answer written by hand (two headings, none, the same ingredient under two, a split line, a heading with a colon or markdown, the "Ingredients" title, a heading that isn't text); a live reading had no outside network to run against, so whether the model keeps every heading on a real page is unverified. (c) A step shares out an ingredient's groups by the order the groups show, which fits recipes that list their groups in the order they are used; a step that names "the sauce" without naming the ingredient lists nothing, as before. (d) A new line in the form starts in the last group (the one just above Add ingredient, or the group of the line before after "Add another") and says so; it can be moved in its editor. (e) Renaming a group to another group's name makes them one, which the editor says before Done, and that can't be undone except by moving lines back.

24. **Voice commands: a few things are unverified or accepted.** (a) Everything was driven in headless Chromium with an injected fake `SpeechRecognition` (it records start, stop and abort, fires final and interim results with several guesses, `end`, and the errors `not-allowed`, `service-not-allowed`, `no-speech`, `network` and `audio-capture`) and a fake `speechSynthesis` (it records each reading and its voice and fires `end` after a set time, `interrupted` on cancel, or nothing at all): 142 checks in a scratch script, from the explainer to leaving; what is committed is the grammar (`scripts/checks/pinchVoice.ts`) and the listening machine's restarts, back-off, waiting for an old session's end, the guards and the idle stop (`scripts/checks/speechListener.ts`), both in `npm run check:pinch-voice`. The sound and the first reading are unlocked in the tap that turns voice control on (`navigator.userActivation.isActive` is true at both calls in Chromium); whether that is enough for Safari on iOS to beep for a voice-started timer was not heard on a device. Not verified: a real microphone, the real speech services (Google's, Apple's, Microsoft's in Edge) and how they transcribe these commands, Chrome's own timings for `no-speech` and for ending a long session, Safari on macOS and iOS (and iOS's shorter sessions and its Siri setting), Android's sound at each start, a noisy kitchen, an accent, and real voices reading a step. (b) The commands are English. Recognition listens in English even on a page in another language, so the commands are heard as words; a French recipe read aloud uses the page's language, and an answer ("12 minutes left") uses the same voice. (c) The same command twice within 1.2 s counts once, so "back, back" said quickly moves one step. (d) A bare "pause" or "stop" is a command when it is the recognizer's top guess: "stop" only quiets a ringing timer, so a shout at a child does nothing worse than "No timer is ringing.", but "pause" alone pauses the running timer. (e) "How much X" with no matching line answers aloud with the words searched ("I can't find “people” in this recipe's ingredients.") rather than staying silent, so a question to someone else in the room can draw that answer. (f) An ingredient listed twice is answered with both amounts, each with its group when the recipe groups them ("Garlic: 4 (For the chicken), 2 (For the sauce)"), and with no labels when it doesn't. (g) The mic in the header goes against "the header holds only the back button and the title": it is the one screen-wide switch that has to be reachable on every view of cooking mode, and the shared `Subview` takes it as `headerAction` (documented as never a call to action). (h) The explainer is a modal over the cooking subview, one of the overlays allowed there (like a help note). (i) "Time's up" is not announced by voice: the beep (and its repeats, once a minute until it is dismissed, five at most) and the timer view coming to the front do that, and nothing pauses listening while the beep plays. (j) Voice reaches the timers only through the session's own calls (`startTimer`, `timers.pause`/`resume`/`focus`, `endTimer`, `backToStep`, `goNextFromTimer`, `jumpTo`), so it follows the timer view's rules; instead of the 0.6 s tap guard it has a 2 s one sized to the recognizer's delay (checked by a drive step taken while the card says "Rang just now"), and "next" never lands where the footer has no button; the four `react-hooks/purity` disables (the Finish path's three `Date.now()` reads and the tap guard's) are there because the compiler flags every clock read in the screen once the voice actions are connected, not because those lines run during render. (k) Voice control turns itself off by its own clocks: a command window after ten seconds (a tap opens it again), waiting for "Hey Pinch" after ten minutes without the phrase ("Mic turned off after 10 quiet minutes." for 8 s) and the lock after fifteen minutes ("Mic turned off after 15 minutes."), so the room isn't heard for the rest of the evening; a cook waiting on a long timer without speaking finds it off when the Time's up rings, which still beeps, and taps (a "Hey Pinch" restarts the ten minutes). (l) The chip names the state (Listening, Waiting, the time left) and the text beside it is a hint ("Say “next” or “how much garlic”", "Say “Hey Pinch”"), so the line is still one 24px slot for messages and the footer is the same height as before. (m) "Next" on the ingredient list refuses rather than moving a step, because the list's only button is "Back to the steps" and the list shows no step counter.

25. **Collections and favorites: a few things are decided here, and a few are unverified or accepted.** (a) The UI word is "collections", not "groups" (an ingredient line's group is a different thing on the same screen); it is `COLLECTION_NOUN` and `COLLECTION_NOUN_PLURAL`, so a rename is one edit, and the code keeps `Collection`, `collectionIds` and `collections` (as "container" stays the code word for a fridge box). (b) The filter picks one collection at a time; intersecting two ("Bulk and High-protein") is not offered, since a second pill would mean either "or" or "and" and the counts would be wrong for one of them. (c) Suggestions are High-protein, Quick and Freezer-friendly; Vegetarian is left out because the data can't say it without guessing (§5a.2). A partial protein sum is not trusted even when it already clears 25 g (it is a lower bound, so it would still be true): the suggestion waits until every ingredient's protein is known or typed, so a recipe is never labelled from half its numbers. (d) The 50-collection cap is the client's; a second device can pass it, and the form then only stops offering "New collection". (e) Deleting a collection needs a connection (a transaction per chunk) and leaves everything as it was when it fails; a recipe that gains the collection on another device while the delete runs keeps an id nothing names, which readers ignore. (f) Two devices editing one recipe's collections at once last-write-wins on the whole list, like any field the form owns. (g) The star is field-scoped, so a rename and a star made at the same moment on two devices both land. (h) Recipes are not starrable: the owner said "meals", and a "Favorites" collection does that job for recipes. (i) The drawer's chips and the sheet's count read listed (non-archived) recipes, while the delete re-reads and counts every recipe that names the collection, archived ones too, so the toast can say one more than the row did when an archived recipe is in it. (j) A real second-browser run of the optimistic star (offline queueing) was not tried; the tap guard was checked as a pure function and the write against the emulator. (n) **The wake phrase and the listening ways** were driven with the same fake recognizer and Playwright's clock (78 checks in scratch scripts): a first window and then waiting in wake mode; a window that runs out and one a command extends; the phrase alone, in one breath with a command, inside a window and under an open sheet; "add a pinch of salt", "a pinch", "okay pinch the edges" and a command without the phrase ignored while waiting; kitchen talk quoted in a window and ignored while kept open; a session that ends by itself restarting with no visible change; taps stepping down; ten quiet minutes; the lock counting down, a command at 0:30 left not extending it, ending with its line, and "tap to talk" chosen while locked ending it; the sheet's rows and what each remembers; a refused microphone said once and never retried, then retried from the explainer; no recognizer and an insecure page; leaving while waiting and while locked leaving no timer or session behind; a Time's up with voice waiting (three beeps, a buzz, the view, the 2 s guard) after 24 minutes of waiting kept alive by the phrase; Read steps aloud with the phrase (recognition paused for the reading, the window waiting out the reading); a hidden tab and its return; a plate ("hey pinch next recipe"). What is committed is the matcher and the whole state table (`scripts/checks/pinchWake.ts`) and `onHalt` (`scripts/checks/speechListener.ts`). Not verified: how real recognizers write "Hey Pinch" for different accents and in a loud kitchen (the spellings are a guess from how the word is usually heard), the real rates of false and missed wakes, the battery and data cost of ten minutes of continuous recognition on a phone, how long Chrome and Safari keep a waiting session alive before they end it, and whether iOS keeps one running with the screen dimmed. (o) "Tap to talk" is the default because the owner asked for less continuous listening: the mic is open for ten seconds at a time, and Read steps aloud only reads while the mic is on, so it reads little in tap mode; a cook who wants every step read picks "Say “Hey Pinch”" or the fifteen minutes. (p) A tap on the lit mic always steps down rather than opening a window, so waiting for the phrase is turned off with the mic itself; a window from the sheet-less state is the phrase or two taps.
